**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *an equestrian* (noun) is a person who rides horses.

Relating to horse riding or showing a person on a horse.

1. She trains for *equestrian* events at a stable in Kentucky.
2. An *equestrian* statue of George Washington stands in the park.
3. The resort has an *equestrian* center and a golf course.

---

### equidistant

**Pronunciation:** /ˌiːkwɪˈdɪstənt/ &middot; *ee-kwi-DIS-tuhnt*
**Русский:** равноудалённый
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Takes:** *from* or *between* + noun
**Form:** equi- (equal) + *distant*

At the same distance from two or more places or things.

1. Our town is *equidistant* from Denver and Boulder.
2. Put the chairs at *equidistant* points around the table.
3. The center of a circle is *equidistant* from every point on it.

---

### equilateral

**Pronunciation:** /ˌiːkwɪˈlætərəl/ &middot; *ee-kwi-LAT-uh-ruhl*
**Русский:** равносторонний
**Type:** shape
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** equi- (equal) + *lateral* (side)

Having all sides the same length.

1. Draw an *equilateral* triangle with sides of five inches.
2. Every angle in an *equilateral* triangle is 60 degrees.
3. The triangle is *equilateral*, so you only need to measure one side.

---

### equipped

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈkwɪpt/ &middot; *ih-KWIPT*
**Русский:** оснащённый, оборудованный; подготовленный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — usually with *well*: *better equipped*, *best equipped*
**Takes:** *with* + noun; *to*-infinitive; *for* + noun
**Form:** -ed from *equip*

Equipped has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Having the tools, machines or supplies that are needed.** (оснащённый, оборудованный)

1. The apartment comes with a fully *equipped* kitchen.
2. All the police cars are *equipped* with cameras.

**2. Having the skills or knowledge that are needed.** (подготовленный, способный)

3. Schools are not *equipped* to deal with the problem.
4. After the training, she felt better *equipped* for the job.

---

### equitable

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛkwɪtəbəl/ &middot; *EK-wi-tuh-buhl*
**Русский:** справедливый, равноправный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more equitable*, *most equitable*
**Opposite:** *inequitable*
**Contrast:** *equitable* means fair, not necessarily *equal*: an *equitable* split can give more to the person who needs more.

Fair and reasonable to everyone involved.

1. The goal is an *equitable* distribution of school funding.
2. The divorce settlement was *equitable*.
3. We need a more *equitable* health care system.

---

### equivalent

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈkwɪvələnt/ &middot; *ih-KWIV-uh-luhnt*
**Русский:** эквивалентный, равноценный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable — degree with *roughly* or *exactly*: *roughly equivalent*
**Takes:** *to* + noun
**Preposition patterns:** [equivalent to](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#equivalent-to)
**Contrast:** *the equivalent of* is a noun phrase: *the equivalent of $500*.

Equal in value, amount, meaning or effect.

1. One cup is *equivalent* to about 240 milliliters.
2. She applied with a bachelor's degree or *equivalent* experience.
3. There is no *equivalent* word in English.

---

### equivocal

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈkwɪvəkəl/ &middot; *ih-KWIV-uh-kuhl*
**Русский:** двусмысленный, неоднозначный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more equivocal*, *most equivocal*
**Opposite:** *unequivocal*

Not clear or definite, and possible to understand in more than one way.

1. The senator gave an *equivocal* answer about the tax plan.
2. The results of the study were *equivocal*.
3. His second statement was even more *equivocal* than the first.

---

### erasable

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈreɪsəbəl/ &middot; *ih-RAY-suh-buhl*
**Русский:** стираемый, стирающийся
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -able from *erase*
**Opposite:** *permanent*

Possible to rub out or remove.

1. Use an *erasable* pen for the crossword.
2. The marks on the whiteboard are *erasable*.
3. The calendar has an *erasable* surface.

---

### erect

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈrɛkt/ &middot; *ih-REKT*
**Русский:** прямой, выпрямленный; стоящий торчком, эрегированный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — often after *stand*, *sit*, *hold*: *stand erect*
**Comparison:** more/most — *more erect*, *most erect*
**Contrast:** *They erected a monument* is the verb: to build.

Erect has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Standing or sitting very straight.** (прямой, выпрямленный)

1. The soldiers stood *erect* as the flag went by.
2. Sit more *erect* and your back won't hurt.
3. She has an *erect*, graceful posture.

**2. Of a body part, stiff and raised instead of lying flat.** (стоящий торчком; эрегированный)

4. The dog listened with its ears *erect*.
5. The hair on the cat's back was *erect*.

---

### erectile

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈrɛktəl/ &middot; *ih-REK-tuhl*
**Русский:** эректильный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable

Relating to the ability of a body part, especially the penis, to become stiff.

1. *Erectile* dysfunction can be a side effect of the drug.
2. The doctor asked about any *erectile* problems.
3. *Erectile* tissue fills with blood.

---

### eroded

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈroʊdɪd/ &middot; *ih-ROH-did*
**Русский:** размытый, выветренный; подорванный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more eroded*, *most eroded*
**Form:** -ed from *erode*

Eroded has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Gradually worn away by water, wind or ice.** (размытый, выветренный)

1. The storm left the beach badly *eroded*.
2. The cliffs on the north side are more *eroded* than the ones in the south.

**2. Gradually weakened or reduced.** (подорванный, ослабленный)

3. The scandal left public trust badly *eroded*.
4. Workers are angry about their *eroded* benefits.

---

### erogenous

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈrɑːdʒənəs/ &middot; *ih-RAH-juh-nuhs*
**Русский:** эрогенный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — almost always *erogenous zone*
**Comparison:** not gradable

Of a part of the body, sexually sensitive.

1. The neck is often called an *erogenous* zone.
2. The magazine ran an article about *erogenous* zones.
3. Ears are a surprisingly common *erogenous* zone.

---

### erotic

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈrɑːtɪk/ &middot; *ih-RAH-tik*
**Русский:** эротический
**Type:** quality; also classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more erotic*, *most erotic*

Relating to or causing sexual desire.

1. The museum has a room of ancient *erotic* art.
2. She writes *erotic* novels under a different name.
3. Critics said the remake was more *erotic* than the original.

---

### errant

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛrənt/ &middot; *ER-uhnt*
**Русский:** шальной, сбившийся с курса; заблудший, провинившийся
**Type:** quality
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable

Errant has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Going in the wrong direction or missing the target.** (шальной, сбившийся с курса)

1. An *errant* golf ball broke the kitchen window.
2. The quarterback's *errant* pass was intercepted.

**2. Behaving badly or breaking the rules.** (заблудший, провинившийся)

3. She finally threw out her *errant* husband.
4. The league fined several *errant* players.

---

### erratic

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈrætɪk/ &middot; *ih-RAT-ik*
**Русский:** беспорядочный, непредсказуемый, неустойчивый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more erratic*, *most erratic*
**Contrast:** Not *erotic* (sexual), which sounds similar.

Changing often and without a pattern, so you cannot predict it.

1. Police pulled him over for *erratic* driving.
2. His behavior has become more *erratic* since he stopped taking his medicine.
3. The Wi-Fi in this hotel is *erratic*.

---

### erroneous

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈroʊniəs/ &middot; *ih-ROH-nee-uhs*
**Русский:** ошибочный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ous from *error*

Not correct; based on wrong information.

1. The article contained several *erroneous* statements.
2. The charge on my bill was *erroneous*.
3. They made an *erroneous* assumption about the cost.

---

### error-prone

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛrərˌproʊn/ &middot; *ER-ur-prohn*
**Русский:** подверженный ошибкам, ненадёжный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more error-prone*, *most error-prone*
**Form:** compound — noun + *prone*

Likely to make or contain mistakes.

1. Entering the data by hand is slow and *error-prone*.
2. The old software was more *error-prone* than the new version.
3. Tired workers are *error-prone*.

---

### ersatz

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛrˌsɑːts/ &middot; *ER-sahts*
**Русский:** суррогатный, поддельный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable

Used instead of the real thing, and usually not as good.

1. During the war people drank *ersatz* coffee made from roasted grain.
2. The casino is full of *ersatz* Roman statues.
3. The restaurant's Mexican food felt *ersatz*.

---

### erstwhile

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɜːrstˌwaɪl/ &middot; *URST-wyl*
**Русский:** бывший, прежний
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable

Former; in the past but not now.

1. He's now working with his *erstwhile* rivals.
2. The *erstwhile* factory is now an apartment building.
3. Her *erstwhile* friends stopped calling her.

---

### erudite

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛrjəˌdaɪt/ &middot; *ER-yuh-dyt*
**Русский:** эрудированный, учёный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more erudite*, *most erudite*

Having or showing great knowledge from reading and study.

1. The professor gave an *erudite* lecture on Greek art.
2. Her essays are witty and *erudite*.
3. He was the most *erudite* man I have ever met.

---

### escalating

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛskəleɪtɪŋ/ &middot; *ES-kuh-lay-ting*
**Русский:** растущий, обостряющийся
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more escalating* is rare; the word already means rising
**Form:** -ing from *escalate*

Getting bigger, worse or more serious step by step.

1. Families are struggling with *escalating* rents.
2. The two countries are locked in an *escalating* trade war.
3. Violence in the region is *escalating*.

---

### escaped

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈskeɪpt/ &middot; *ih-SKAYPT*
**Русский:** сбежавший
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only — *The prisoner escaped* is the verb
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *escape*

Having gotten away from a prison, cage or other place.

1. Police are searching for an *escaped* prisoner.
2. An *escaped* python was found in a neighbor's garage.
3. The farmer rounded up the *escaped* cows.

---

### escapist

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈskeɪpɪst/ &middot; *ih-SKAY-pist*
**Русский:** эскапистский, уводящий от реальности
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *escapist fiction*, not *the movie was escapist* in careful writing
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ist from *escape*
**Contrast:** *An escapist* is also a noun — a person who avoids real life.

Offering a pleasant break from real life and its problems.

1. She reads *escapist* novels on the beach.
2. It's pure *escapist* entertainment, and that's the point.
3. The studio built its name on big, loud, *escapist* movies.

---

### esoteric

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛsəˈtɛrɪk/ &middot; *es-uh-TER-ik*
**Русский:** эзотерический, понятный немногим
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more esoteric*, *most esoteric*
**Opposite:** *mainstream*

Understood by only a small group with special knowledge.

1. The paper is full of *esoteric* mathematical notation.
2. His interests are pretty *esoteric* — medieval bookbinding, mostly.
3. The second half of the lecture was even more *esoteric* than the first.

---

### established

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈstæblɪʃt/ &middot; *ih-STAB-lisht*
**Русский:** устоявшийся, признанный; давно работающий
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more established*, *most established*
**Form:** -ed from *establish*

Existing for a long time and generally accepted or respected.

1. This is an *established* law firm with offices in three cities.
2. It is an *established* fact that the drug lowers blood pressure.
3. She is far more *established* now than she was ten years ago.

---

### esteemed

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈstiːmd/ &middot; *ih-STEEMD*
**Русский:** уважаемый, почитаемый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *our esteemed guest*
**Comparison:** more/most — *more esteemed*, *most esteemed*
**Form:** -ed from *esteem*
**Contrast:** Formal and often a little ironic in speech: *my esteemed colleague* can be a dig.

Greatly respected and admired.

1. Please welcome our *esteemed* guest from Tokyo.
2. He studied under an *esteemed* professor of physics.
3. She is the *most esteemed* judge on the panel.

---

### estimated

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛstɪmeɪtɪd/ &middot; *ES-tuh-may-tid*
**Русский:** предполагаемый, оценочный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *the estimated cost*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *estimate*

Calculated roughly, not counted exactly.

1. The *estimated* cost of the repair is $1,200.
2. An *estimated* 40,000 people attended the concert.
3. Your *estimated* arrival time is 6:15 p.m.

---

### Estonian

**Pronunciation:** /ɛˈstoʊniən/ &middot; *es-TOH-nee-uhn*
**Русский:** эстонский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Also a noun — a person (*an Estonian*) or the language (*She speaks Estonian*).

From Estonia, or belonging to its people and language.

1. We stayed with an *Estonian* family in Tallinn.
2. The menu gave an *Estonian* name for every dish.
3. *Estonian* winters are long and dark.

---

### estranged

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈstreɪndʒd/ &middot; *ih-STRAYNJD*
**Русский:** отчуждённый, порвавший отношения
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more estranged*, *most estranged*
**Takes:** *from* + noun
**Form:** -ed from *estrange*

No longer friendly or in contact with a family member or partner.

1. He lives with his *estranged* wife's parents.
2. She has been *estranged* from her father for years.
3. The brothers grew more *estranged* after the funeral.

---

### etched

**Pronunciation:** /ɛtʃt/ &middot; *ECHT*
**Русский:** гравированный; врезавшийся (в память)
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Takes:** *in*, *into* or *on* + noun
**Form:** -ed participle of *etch*

Etched has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Cut or marked into a hard surface.** (гравированный, вытравленный)

1. The front door had an *etched* glass panel.
2. Their initials were *etched* on the trophy.

**2. Fixed so clearly that it cannot be forgotten or hidden.** (врезавшийся (в память), застывший (на лице))

3. That night is *etched* in my memory.
4. Worry was *etched* into his face.

---

### eternal

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈtɜːrnəl/ &middot; *ih-TUR-nuhl*
**Русский:** вечный; бесконечный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable — something either lasts forever or does not
**Opposite:** *temporary*

Eternal has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Lasting forever, with no beginning and no end.** (вечный)

1. They believe in *eternal* life.
2. An *eternal* flame burns at the memorial.
3. Nothing in this world is *eternal*.

**2. Seeming to go on and on, in an annoying way.** (бесконечный, нескончаемый)

4. I'm tired of your *eternal* complaining.
5. There is the *eternal* question of who pays for it.

---

### ethereal

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈθɪriəl/ &middot; *ih-THEER-ee-uhl*
**Русский:** неземной, воздушный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more ethereal*, *most ethereal*

Light, delicate and beautiful in a way that seems not of this world.

1. The singer has an *ethereal* voice.
2. Morning fog gave the lake an *ethereal* look.
3. Her paintings are even more *ethereal* in person.

---

### ethical

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛθɪkəl/ &middot; *ETH-i-kuhl*
**Русский:** этичный, нравственный; этический
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more ethical*, *most ethical*
**Form:** -al from *ethic*
**Opposite:** *unethical*

Ethical has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Morally right, or behaving according to moral rules.** (этичный, нравственный)

1. It isn't *ethical* to read someone else's mail.
2. They promise *ethical* treatment of the animals.
3. This company is far *more ethical* than its competitors.

**2. Having to do with questions of right and wrong.** (этический)

4. The study raises serious *ethical* questions.
5. Every doctor here signs an *ethical* code.

---

### Ethiopian

**Pronunciation:** /ˌiːθiˈoʊpiən/ &middot; *ee-thee-OH-pee-uhn*
**Русский:** эфиопский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Also a noun: *two Ethiopians*.

From Ethiopia, or belonging to its people and culture.

1. We had dinner at an *Ethiopian* restaurant downtown.
2. *Ethiopian* coffee is famous all over the world.
3. Both of her parents are *Ethiopian*.

---

### ethnic

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛθnɪk/ &middot; *ETH-nik*
**Русский:** этнический, национальный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *an ethnic minority*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Of people and culture, not of a country: a citizen of Russia may be *ethnic* Korean.

Having to do with a group that shares a culture, language or origin.

1. The country has more than a hundred *ethnic* groups.
2. *Ethnic* tension in the region goes back centuries.
3. She grew up in an *ethnic* neighborhood on the South Side.

---

### ethnocentric

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛθnoʊˈsɛntrɪk/ &middot; *eth-noh-SEN-trik*
**Русский:** этноцентричный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more ethnocentric*, *most ethnocentric*
**Form:** *ethno-* + *-centric*

Judging other peoples by the standards of your own, as if yours were the normal ones.

1. The old textbook takes an *ethnocentric* view of history.
2. His remarks about the food were plainly *ethnocentric*.
3. The course asks students to notice their own *ethnocentric* assumptions.

---

### etymological

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛtɪməˈlɑːdʒɪkəl/ &middot; *et-i-muh-LOJ-i-kuhl*
**Русский:** этимологический
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** from *etymology* — the history of words

Having to do with where words come from and how they changed.

1. The dictionary gives an *etymological* note for each entry.
2. There is no *etymological* link between the two words.
3. He made an *etymological* argument for the older spelling.

---

### euphemistic

**Pronunciation:** /ˌjuːfəˈmɪstɪk/ &middot; *yoo-fuh-MIS-tik*
**Русский:** эвфемистический, смягчённый
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more euphemistic*, *most euphemistic*
**Form:** -ic from *euphemism*

Using a mild or vague word in place of a blunt one.

1. "Letting people go" is a *euphemistic* way of saying "firing them."
2. The report is written in *euphemistic* corporate language.
3. His answer was *euphemistic* and told us nothing.

---

### euphoric

**Pronunciation:** /juːˈfɔːrɪk/ &middot; *yoo-FOR-ik*
**Русский:** эйфорический, ликующий
**Type:** emotion
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more euphoric*, *most euphoric*
**Form:** -ic from *euphoria*

Feeling or showing very great happiness and excitement.

1. The fans were *euphoric* after the win.
2. A *euphoric* crowd filled the square.
3. She felt *euphoric* for about a day, and then the work began.

---

### Eurasian

**Pronunciation:** /jʊˈreɪʒən/ &middot; *yuu-RAY-zhuhn*
**Русский:** евразийский; европейско-азиатского происхождения
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable

Eurasian has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Of Europe and Asia taken together.** (евразийский)

1. The *Eurasian* land mass runs from Portugal to Japan.
2. *Eurasian* trade routes crossed these mountains.

**2. Of mixed European and Asian descent.** (имеющий европейско-азиатские корни)

3. Her mother is *Eurasian*.
4. The port city had a large *Eurasian* community.

---

### Eurocentric

**Pronunciation:** /ˌjʊroʊˈsɛntrɪk/ &middot; *yuu-roh-SEN-trik*
**Русский:** европоцентричный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more Eurocentric*, *most Eurocentric*
**Form:** *Euro-* + *-centric*

Treating Europe and its culture as the center of everything.

1. Students complained that the reading list was *Eurocentric*.
2. He offers a less *Eurocentric* account of the war.
3. The museum is rewriting its *Eurocentric* labels.

---

### evangelical

**Pronunciation:** /ˌiːvænˈdʒɛlɪkəl/ &middot; *ee-van-JEL-i-kuhl*
**Русский:** евангелический; страстно убеждённый
**Type:** classifying; also quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more evangelical*, *most evangelical* (the 'zealous' sense)
**Contrast:** Capitalized as a noun for the movement: *Evangelicals voted in large numbers*.

Evangelical has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Belonging to the Protestant churches that stress the Bible and personal conversion.** (евангелический)

1. She was raised in an *evangelical* church in Texas.
2. *Evangelical* voters were a large part of the turnout.

**2. Trying eagerly to convince other people of a belief.** (страстно убеждённый, проповеднический)

3. He is *evangelical* about cold showers.
4. The company has an almost *evangelical* faith in its own product.

---

### evaporated

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈvæpəreɪtɪd/ &middot; *ih-VAP-uh-ray-tid*
**Русский:** выпаренный, сгущённый (о молоке)
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *evaporated milk*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *evaporate*
**Contrast:** *Evaporated milk* is unsweetened; *condensed milk* is sweet — a common mix-up in recipes.

Made thicker by having most of the water removed.

1. The recipe calls for a can of *evaporated* milk.
2. Stir the *evaporated* milk into the pumpkin mixture.
3. You can substitute cream for *evaporated* milk in a pinch.

---

### evasive

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈveɪsɪv/ &middot; *ih-VAY-siv*
**Русский:** уклончивый; уклоняющийся
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more evasive*, *most evasive*
**Takes:** *about* + noun or *-ing*
**Form:** -ive from *evade*
**Opposite:** *straightforward*

Evasive has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Avoiding a direct answer.** (уклончивый)

1. The witness gave *evasive* answers all morning.
2. He was *evasive* about where the money came from.
3. She got *more evasive* the longer we talked.

**2. Meant to get out of the way of danger.** (уклоняющийся, манёвренный)

4. The pilot took *evasive* action.
5. The deer made an *evasive* jump into the trees.

---

### even

**Pronunciation:** /ˈiːvən/ &middot; *EE-vuhn*
**Русский:** ровный; равный; чётный; спокойный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more even*, *most even* (the 'level' and 'steady' senses)
**Opposite:** *uneven*; *odd* (of numbers)
**Contrast:** *Even* is also a very common adverb: *even better*, *even if*. That is not this adjective.

Even has 4 distinct senses.

**1. Flat, level and smooth.** (ровный, гладкий)

1. Find an *even* surface for the tripod.
2. The new concrete is much *more even* than the old walkway.

**2. Equal in number, amount or score, so that nobody owes anything.** (равный, поровну)

3. Split the check into *even* shares.
4. You bought lunch, so now we're *even*.

**3. Of a number, able to be divided by two.** (чётный)

5. Houses with *even* numbers are on this side of the street.
6. Two, four and six are *even* numbers.

**4. Steady and calm, without sudden changes.** (спокойный, ровный)

7. She answered in an *even* voice.
8. Keep the oven at an *even* temperature.

---

### even-handed

**Pronunciation:** /ˌiːvənˈhændɪd/ &middot; *ee-vuhn-HAN-did*
**Русский:** беспристрастный, справедливый
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more even-handed*, *most even-handed*
**Form:** compound — *even* + *hand* + -ed
**Opposite:** *biased*

Treating everyone fairly and in the same way.

1. The judge is known for her *even-handed* rulings.
2. He was *even-handed* with both of his children.
3. This is the *most even-handed* account of the war I have read.

---

### even-tempered

**Pronunciation:** /ˌiːvənˈtɛmpərd/ &middot; *ee-vuhn-TEM-purd*
**Русский:** уравновешенный, спокойный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more even-tempered*, *most even-tempered*
**Form:** compound — *even* + *temper* + -ed
**Opposite:** *hot-tempered*

Calm by nature and not easily made angry.

1. He is an *even-tempered* man who rarely raises his voice.
2. Golden retrievers are usually *even-tempered*.
3. She is *more even-tempered* than anyone else on the team.

---

### evenhanded

**Pronunciation:** /ˌiːvənˈhændɪd/ &middot; *ee-vuhn-HAN-did*
**Русский:** беспристрастный, справедливый
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more evenhanded*, *most evenhanded*
**Form:** compound — *even* + *handed*
**Opposite:** *biased*
**Contrast:** Also written *even-handed*.

Treating both sides fairly and equally.

1. The judge was *evenhanded* with both attorneys.
2. We need a more *evenhanded* review of the complaints.
3. Her coverage of the campaign was *evenhanded*.

---

### eventful

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈvɛntfəl/ &middot; *ih-VENT-fuhl*
**Русский:** насыщенный событиями, богатый событиями
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more eventful*, *most eventful*
**Form:** -ful from *event*
**Opposite:** *uneventful*

Full of interesting or important things happening.

1. We had an *eventful* week in Chicago.
2. Her life has been *eventful*, to say the least.
3. That was the *most eventful* game of the season.

---

### ever-changing

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛvərˈtʃeɪndʒɪŋ/ &middot; *ev-ur-CHAYN-jing*
**Русский:** постоянно меняющийся
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *the ever-changing rules*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — *ever* + *changing*

Always becoming different, never staying the same.

1. Teachers face an *ever-changing* set of state requirements.
2. The *ever-changing* light on the water is what drew him to paint here.
3. It is hard to keep up with the *ever-changing* tax code.

---

### ever-present

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛvərˈprɛzənt/ &middot; *ev-ur-PREZ-uhnt*
**Русский:** постоянный, вездесущий
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *the ever-present risk*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — *ever* + *present*

Always there, and never going away.

1. Wildfire is an *ever-present* danger in this valley.
2. He walked in with his *ever-present* coffee cup.
3. There is the *ever-present* question of who will pay.

---

### evergreen

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛvərɡriːn/ &middot; *EV-ur-green*
**Русский:** вечнозелёный; не теряющий актуальности
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — *ever* + *green*
**Opposite:** *deciduous*
**Contrast:** *An evergreen* is also a noun — the tree itself: *We planted three evergreens*.

Evergreen has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Of a tree or bush, keeping its green leaves all year.** (вечнозелёный)

1. Pines and firs are *evergreen*.
2. A row of *evergreen* shrubs hides the fence.

**2. Staying useful or popular no matter how much time passes.** (не теряющий актуальности, вечный)

3. "How to save for retirement" is *evergreen* content.
4. It's an *evergreen* question in American politics.

---

### everlasting

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛvərˈlæstɪŋ/ &middot; *ev-ur-LAS-ting*
**Русский:** вечный, нескончаемый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *everlasting life*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — *ever* + *lasting*

Lasting forever, or seeming to.

1. The hymn promises *everlasting* life.
2. He has my *everlasting* gratitude.
3. I'm tired of their *everlasting* arguments about money.

---

### everyday

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛvrideɪ/ &middot; *EV-ree-day*
**Русский:** повседневный, обыденный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *everyday life*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — *every* + *day*
**Contrast:** One word before a noun; two words as a time phrase: *everyday problems* but *I see him every day*.

Ordinary and happening as part of normal life.

1. The book is about the *everyday* lives of factory workers.
2. These are *everyday* dishes, not the good china.
3. Spanish is her *everyday* language at home.

---

### evidence-based

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛvɪdənsbeɪst/ &middot; *EV-i-duhns-bayst*
**Русский:** основанный на доказательствах, доказательный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *evidence-based medicine*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — noun + participle, like *market-based*

Built on research and proof rather than on opinion or habit.

1. The clinic uses only *evidence-based* treatments.
2. She wants *evidence-based* policy, not guesswork.
3. The district adopted an *evidence-based* reading program.

---

### evil

**Pronunciation:** /ˈiːvəl/ &middot; *EE-vuhl*
**Русский:** злой, злонамеренный; дурной
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est or more/most — *eviler* or *more evil*, *evilest* or *most evil*
**Opposite:** *good*
**Contrast:** *Evil* is also a noun: *the problem of evil*, *a necessary evil*.

Evil has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Deeply bad in a moral way, and wanting to harm others.** (злой, злонамеренный)

1. He plays the *evil* wizard in the new movie.
2. No one is born *evil*.
3. She is the *most evil* character in the whole series.

**2. Very unpleasant, especially to the nose.** (отвратительный, мерзкий)

4. An *evil* smell came from the basement.
5. He gave me an *evil* look and walked out.

---

### evocative

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈvɑːkətɪv/ &middot; *ih-VAH-kuh-tiv*
**Русский:** вызывающий воспоминания, выразительный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more evocative*, *most evocative*
**Takes:** *of* + noun
**Form:** -ive from *evoke*

Bringing strong pictures, memories or feelings to mind.

1. The book has an *evocative* description of a Brooklyn summer.
2. The music is *evocative* of the 1970s.
3. His photographs are *more evocative* than any of the text.

---

### evolutionary

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛvəˈluːʃənɛri/ &middot; *ev-uh-LOO-shuh-ner-ee*
**Русский:** эволюционный; постепенный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *evolutionary biology*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ary from *evolution*
**Contrast:** In business writing it means *gradual*, the opposite of *revolutionary*.

Evolutionary has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Having to do with the slow development of living things over time.** (эволюционный)

1. She teaches *evolutionary* biology at Michigan.
2. Color vision gave early primates an *evolutionary* advantage.

**2. Happening by small steps rather than all at once.** (постепенный, эволюционный)

3. The new phone is *evolutionary*, not revolutionary.
4. They favor an *evolutionary* approach to reform.

---

### ex officio

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛks əˈfɪʃioʊ/ &middot; *eks uh-FISH-ee-oh*
**Русский:** по должности, в силу занимаемой должности
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — before a noun, or right after it: *a member ex officio*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** Latin — "from the office"
**Contrast:** Also an adverb: *She serves ex officio.* Written as two words, no hyphen.

Holding a position automatically because of another job you hold.

1. The mayor is an *ex officio* member of the board.
2. *Ex officio* members may speak but not vote.
3. As treasurer, she has an *ex officio* seat on every committee.

---

### exact

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzækt/ &middot; *ig-ZAKT*
**Русский:** точный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exact*, *most exact*
**Opposite:** *approximate*
**Contrast:** *Exact* is also a formal verb — *to exact a price*. And *exacting* means "demanding," not "precise."

Completely correct and with no error in the details.

1. What were his *exact* words?
2. I need the *exact* measurements before I cut the wood.
3. Her figures are *more exact* than the ones in the first report.

---

### exacting

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzæktɪŋ/ &middot; *ig-ZAK-ting*
**Русский:** требовательный, взыскательный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exacting*, *most exacting*
**Form:** -ing from *exact* (the verb)
**Contrast:** Not "precise." An *exacting* boss demands a lot; an *exact* figure is correct to the decimal.

Demanding a great deal of care, effort or skill.

1. Watchmaking is *exacting* work.
2. She is an *exacting* editor who questions every comma.
3. The standards here are *more exacting* than at my last job.

---

### exaggerated

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzædʒəreɪtɪd/ &middot; *ig-ZAJ-uh-ray-tid*
**Русский:** преувеличенный; нарочитый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exaggerated*, *most exaggerated*
**Form:** -ed from *exaggerate*

Exaggerated has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Made to seem larger, better or worse than it really is.** (преувеличенный)

1. Reports of the damage were *exaggerated*.
2. He has an *exaggerated* idea of his own importance.
3. The second telling was even *more exaggerated* than the first.

**2. Larger than normal on purpose, for effect.** (нарочитый, подчёркнутый)

4. The actor gave an *exaggerated* bow.
5. The jacket has *exaggerated* shoulders.

---

### exalted

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɔːltɪd/ &middot; *ig-ZAWL-tid*
**Русский:** высокий (о положении); возвышенный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exalted*, *most exalted*
**Form:** -ed from *exalt*

Exalted has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Very high in rank or importance.** (высокий, высокопоставленный)

1. He rose to the *exalted* position of chief justice.
2. From her *exalted* seat on the board, she rarely met a customer.
3. Few writers reach such an *exalted* place in the language.

**2. Noble and full of high feeling.** (возвышенный)

4. The speech was written in an *exalted* style.
5. She left the concert in an *exalted* mood.

---

### exasperated

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzæspəreɪtɪd/ &middot; *ig-ZAS-puh-ray-tid*
**Русский:** раздражённый, выведенный из себя
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exasperated*, *most exasperated*
**Takes:** *with* or *by* + noun
**Form:** -ed from *exasperate*
**Contrast:** *-ed* is the person who feels it; *-ing* is what causes it: *I was exasperated by his exasperating questions*.

Very annoyed, especially after trying to be patient for a long time.

1. She let out an *exasperated* sigh.
2. I'm *exasperated* with the whole process.
3. By the fourth phone call he was *more exasperated* than angry.

---

### exasperating

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzæspəreɪtɪŋ/ &middot; *ig-ZAS-puh-ray-ting*
**Русский:** раздражающий, выводящий из себя
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exasperating*, *most exasperating*
**Form:** -ing from *exasperate*

Making someone very annoyed.

1. Waiting on hold for an hour is *exasperating*.
2. He has an *exasperating* habit of answering a question with a question.
3. The form is *more exasperating* than the tax itself.

---

### exceptional

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsɛpʃənəl/ &middot; *ik-SEP-shuh-nuhl*
**Русский:** исключительный, выдающийся; редкий
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exceptional*, *most exceptional*
**Form:** -al from *exception*
**Opposite:** *ordinary*

Exceptional has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Much better than usual.** (исключительный, выдающийся)

1. The service at that restaurant is *exceptional*.
2. She is an *exceptional* student.
3. This year's crop was the *most exceptional* in a decade.

**2. Unusual, and not what normally happens.** (редкий, исключительный)

4. We make refunds only in *exceptional* cases.
5. The delay was due to *exceptional* weather.

---

### excess

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛksɛs/ &middot; *EK-ses*
**Русский:** избыточный, лишний
**Type:** quantity
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *excess baggage*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *Excess* names the extra amount itself (*excess weight*); *excessive* judges it as too much (*excessive force*).

More than the amount that is allowed or needed.

1. The airline charges $75 for *excess* baggage.
2. Drain off any *excess* fat before adding the onions.
3. You can roll *excess* contributions into next year.

---

### excessive

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsɛsɪv/ &middot; *ik-SES-iv*
**Русский:** чрезмерный, непомерный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more excessive*, *most excessive*
**Form:** -ive from *excess*
**Contrast:** *Excessive* always judges the amount as too much; *excess* only says there is extra.

Much more than is reasonable or acceptable.

1. The officer was fired for *excessive* force.
2. Ten dollars for a coffee seems *excessive*.
3. Their spending grew *more excessive* every year.

---

### exchangeable

**Pronunciation:** /ɪksˈtʃeɪndʒəbəl/ &middot; *iks-CHAYN-juh-buhl*
**Русский:** подлежащий обмену, обмениваемый
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Takes:** *for* + noun
**Form:** -able from *exchange*

Able to be traded for something else.

1. Sale items are not *exchangeable*.
2. The points are *exchangeable* for flights or hotel nights.
3. Keep the receipt, or the gift won't be *exchangeable*.

---

### excitable

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsaɪtəbəl/ &middot; *ik-SY-tuh-buhl*
**Русский:** легко возбудимый, впечатлительный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more excitable*, *most excitable*
**Form:** -able from *excite*

Becoming excited or upset very easily.

1. The puppy is friendly but *excitable*.
2. He's an *excitable* kid who can't sit still.
3. She gets *more excitable* the closer we get to a deadline.

---

### exclusive

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈskluːsɪv/ &middot; *ik-SKLOO-siv*
**Русский:** эксклюзивный; элитный; взаимоисключающий
**Type:** classifying; also quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exclusive*, *most exclusive* (the 'elite' sense)
**Takes:** *to* + noun
**Contrast:** *An exclusive* is also a noun in journalism — a story only one outlet has.

Exclusive has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Available to one person or group and to nobody else.** (эксклюзивный, исключительный)

1. The magazine has an *exclusive* interview with the director.
2. This offer is *exclusive* to members.

**2. Expensive, and open only to people the owners choose.** (элитный, закрытый)

3. They joined an *exclusive* country club.
4. It's the *most exclusive* address in the city.

**3. Unable to be true or to happen at the same time as something else.** (взаимоисключающий)

5. The two goals are not mutually *exclusive*.
6. These categories are *exclusive*, so pick only one.

---

### excruciating

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈskruːʃieɪtɪŋ/ &middot; *ik-SKROO-shee-ay-ting*
**Русский:** мучительный, невыносимый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more excruciating*, *most excruciating*
**Contrast:** Often a joke about boredom rather than pain: *an excruciating three-hour meeting*.

Excruciating has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Causing extreme pain.** (мучительный, нестерпимый)

1. The pain in his back was *excruciating*.
2. She suffered an *excruciating* migraine for two days.

**2. Extremely boring, embarrassing or hard to sit through.** (невыносимый, мучительный)

3. The wedding toast was *excruciating*.
4. He described every detail in *excruciating* detail.

---

### exculpatory

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈskʌlpətɔːri/ &middot; *ik-SKUL-puh-tor-ee*
**Русский:** оправдательный, снимающий вину
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — usually before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** from Latin *culpa* — "fault"
**Opposite:** *incriminating*
**Contrast:** Legal English, almost always about evidence.

Showing that someone is not guilty.

1. The defense found *exculpatory* evidence buried in the files.
2. Prosecutors must hand over any *exculpatory* material.
3. Nothing in the emails was *exculpatory*.

---

### excusable

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈskjuːzəbəl/ &middot; *ik-SKYOO-zuh-buhl*
**Русский:** простительный, извинительный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more excusable*, *most excusable*
**Form:** -able from *excuse*
**Opposite:** *inexcusable*

Not too bad to be forgiven.

1. A first mistake is *excusable*.
2. Being late once is *excusable*; being late every day is not.
3. Her sharp answer was *more excusable* than it sounded, given the week she'd had.

---

### executable

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛksɪkjuːtəbəl/ &middot; *EK-si-kyoo-tuh-buhl*
**Русский:** исполняемый (о файле)
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -able from *execute*
**Contrast:** *An executable* is also a noun in computing — the program file itself.

Of a computer file, able to be run as a program.

1. Don't open *executable* attachments from strangers.
2. The installer ships as a single *executable* file.
3. Make the script *executable* before you run it.

---

### executive

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɛkjətɪv/ &middot; *ig-ZEK-yuh-tiv*
**Русский:** исполнительный, руководящий
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *an executive decision*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *An executive* is far more common as a noun — a senior manager: *She's a bank executive*.

Having the power to run an organization and make decisions.

1. The president signed an *executive* order.
2. I made an *executive* decision and ordered pizza for everyone.
3. She sits on the *executive* committee.

---

### exemplary

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɛmpləri/ &middot; *ig-ZEM-pluh-ree*
**Русский:** образцовый, примерный; показательный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exemplary*, *most exemplary*
**Form:** -ary from *example*

Exemplary has 2 distinct senses.

**1. So good that it should serve as a model for others.** (образцовый, примерный)

1. His military service was *exemplary*.
2. She keeps an *exemplary* set of records.
3. Of all the applications, hers was the *most exemplary*.

**2. Of a punishment, severe, so as to warn other people.** (показательный)

4. The jury awarded *exemplary* damages.
5. The court wanted an *exemplary* sentence in this case.

---

### exhilarated

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɪləreɪtɪd/ &middot; *ig-ZIL-uh-ray-tid*
**Русский:** воодушевлённый, в приподнятом настроении
**Type:** emotion
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exhilarated*, *most exhilarated*
**Takes:** *by* + noun
**Form:** -ed — how the person feels; *exhilarating* is what causes it
**Contrast:** The person is *exhilarated*; the thing is *exhilarating*: an *exhilarating* ride leaves you *exhilarated*.

Excited and full of happy energy.

1. We came off the roller coaster *exhilarated*.
2. She felt *exhilarated* by the crowd's reaction.
3. The runners looked tired but *exhilarated*.

---

### exhilarating

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɪləreɪtɪŋ/ &middot; *ig-ZIL-uh-ray-ting*
**Русский:** волнующий, бодрящий
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exhilarating*, *most exhilarating*
**Form:** -ing from *exhilarate*

Making you feel very happy, awake and full of energy.

1. The ride down the mountain was *exhilarating*.
2. There's something *exhilarating* about a cold morning swim.
3. Her first week in New York was the *most exhilarating* of her life.

---

### exiled

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛksaɪld/ &middot; *EK-syld*
**Русский:** изгнанный, живущий в изгнании
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Takes:** *from* + noun; *to* + place
**Form:** -ed from *exile*

Forced to live away from your own country.

1. The *exiled* king settled in Portugal.
2. She was *exiled* from her homeland at nineteen.
3. *Exiled* writers kept the language alive abroad.

---

### existential

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛɡzɪˈstɛnʃəl/ &middot; *eg-zi-STEN-shuhl*
**Русский:** экзистенциальный; угрожающий самому существованию
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *an existential threat*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -al from *existence*

Existential has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Threatening the very existence of something.** (угрожающий самому существованию)

1. Climate change is an *existential* threat to the islands.
2. The lawsuit is an *existential* risk for the company.

**2. Having to do with the meaning and purpose of human life.** (экзистенциальный)

3. He's having an *existential* crisis at forty.
4. The novel asks *existential* questions about free will.

---

### existing

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɪstɪŋ/ &middot; *ig-ZIS-ting*
**Русский:** существующий, нынешний, действующий
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *the existing rules*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *exist*
**Contrast:** Use *existing* for what is already there, *existent* almost never — the common word is its opposite, *nonexistent*.

Present now, before any change is made.

1. The *existing* law already covers this.
2. We'll connect the new wing to the *existing* building.
3. *Existing* customers keep their old rate.

---

### exonerated

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɑːnəreɪtɪd/ &middot; *ig-ZAH-nuh-ray-tid*
**Русский:** оправданный, реабилитированный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Takes:** *of* + noun
**Form:** -ed from *exonerate*
**Contrast:** *Exonerated* means cleared of blame; *acquitted* only means the jury did not convict.

Officially cleared of blame for something.

1. He was fully *exonerated* after new evidence appeared.
2. The *exonerated* men spent nineteen years in prison.
3. She was *exonerated* of any wrongdoing by the review board.

---

### exorbitant

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɔːrbɪtənt/ &middot; *ig-ZOR-bi-tuhnt*
**Русский:** непомерный, заоблачный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exorbitant*, *most exorbitant*
**Contrast:** Almost always about money — prices, rent, fees — not about size or effort.

Of a price or charge, far too high.

1. They charge *exorbitant* fees for a simple wire transfer.
2. Rent downtown is *exorbitant*.
3. The bar's prices are *more exorbitant* than the restaurant's.

---

### exotic

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɑːtɪk/ &middot; *ig-ZAH-tik*
**Русский:** экзотический, диковинный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exotic*, *most exotic*

Exotic has 2 distinct senses.

**1. From a distant country, and unusual or exciting because of that.** (экзотический, заморский)

1. The market sells *exotic* fruit you can't find in stores.
2. They keep *exotic* pets — a python and two parrots.
3. Hawaii sounded *exotic* to a kid from Ohio.

**2. Strikingly unusual or striking in appearance.** (диковинный, необычный)

4. She has an *exotic* taste in hats.
5. The *most exotic* thing on the menu was the octopus.

---

### expandable

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspændəbəl/ &middot; *ik-SPAN-duh-buhl*
**Русский:** расширяемый, раздвижной
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -able from *expand*

Able to be made larger.

1. The suitcase has an *expandable* section for the trip home.
2. Storage is *expandable* up to two terabytes.
3. We bought an *expandable* dining table.

---

### expanded

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspændɪd/ &middot; *ik-SPAN-did*
**Русский:** расширенный, увеличенный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed participle of *expand*
**Opposite:** *reduced*

Made larger or wider than before.

1. The paper ran an *expanded* version of the story.
2. The *expanded* stadium seats sixty thousand.
3. She took on an *expanded* role after the merger.

---

### expansionist

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspænʃənɪst/ &middot; *ik-SPAN-shuh-nist*
**Русский:** экспансионистский
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *expansionist policies*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ist from *expansion*
**Contrast:** *An expansionist* is also a noun — a person who favors such a policy.

Aiming to make a country's or company's territory or power larger.

1. The empire pursued *expansionist* policies for a century.
2. Neighbors watched his *expansionist* ambitions with alarm.
3. The chain's *expansionist* strategy added 300 stores in two years.

---

### expansive

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspænsɪv/ &middot; *ik-SPAN-siv*
**Русский:** обширный; общительный, открытый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more expansive*, *most expansive*
**Form:** -ive from *expand*

Expansive has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Covering a wide area, or wide in scope.** (обширный, просторный)

1. The house has an *expansive* view of the bay.
2. She gave an *expansive* answer that covered the whole century.

**2. Friendly, talkative and willing to say a lot.** (общительный, открытый)

3. After a glass of wine he became *expansive*.
4. The coach was *more expansive* with reporters than usual.

---

### expatriate

**Pronunciation:** /ɛksˈpeɪtriət/ &middot; *eks-PAY-tree-uht*
**Русский:** живущий за границей, экспатриантский
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *the expatriate community*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Usually a noun, and usually shortened: *She's an expat in Lisbon*. Not the same as *immigrant*, though the difference is mostly about who is speaking.

Living outside your own country, usually for work.

1. There's a large *expatriate* community in Dubai.
2. The school serves *expatriate* families.
3. He writes for an *expatriate* newspaper in Paris.

---

### expectant

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspɛktənt/ &middot; *ik-SPEK-tuhnt*
**Русский:** ожидающий, выжидательный; беременная
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more expectant*, *most expectant*
**Form:** -ant from *expect*

Expectant has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Looking as if you are waiting for something good to happen.** (ожидающий, выжидательный)

1. The dog sat by the door with an *expectant* look.
2. An *expectant* hush fell over the theater.
3. The crowd grew *more expectant* as the hour approached.

**2. Pregnant, or about to become a parent.** (беременная; будущий (об отце))

4. The class is for *expectant* mothers.
5. *Expectant* fathers are welcome too.

---

### expected

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspɛktɪd/ &middot; *ik-SPEK-tid*
**Русский:** ожидаемый, предполагаемый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *expect*
**Opposite:** *unexpected*

Thought likely to happen or to arrive.

1. The *expected* delivery date is Friday.
2. His reaction was entirely *expected*.
3. Turnout was lower than *expected*.

---

### expedient

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspiːdiənt/ &middot; *ik-SPEE-dee-uhnt*
**Русский:** целесообразный, выгодный (но не всегда честный)
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more expedient*, *most expedient*
**Contrast:** Not "fast." *Expedient* means useful right now, often at the cost of what is right — that is *expeditious*.

Useful or convenient in a situation, though perhaps not honest or fair.

1. It was politically *expedient* to blame the previous administration.
2. They took the *expedient* route and settled out of court.
3. Firing him would be *more expedient* than fixing the process.

---

### expedited

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛkspɪdaɪtɪd/ &middot; *EK-spuh-dy-tid*
**Русский:** ускоренный, срочный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *expedited shipping*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *expedite*

Handled faster than by the normal process.

1. Pay $20 extra for *expedited* shipping.
2. She applied for *expedited* passport service.
3. The court granted an *expedited* review of the case.

---

### expeditious

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛkspəˈdɪʃəs/ &middot; *ek-spuh-DISH-uhs*
**Русский:** быстрый, оперативный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more expeditious*, *most expeditious*
**Contrast:** Formal, and about speed. *Expedient* is about convenience, not speed — do not swap them.

Done quickly and efficiently.

1. We ask for an *expeditious* response to the complaint.
2. Mailing the form is the *most expeditious* way to do it.
3. Their handling of the recall was *expeditious*.

---

### expendable

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspɛndəbəl/ &middot; *ik-SPEN-duh-buhl*
**Русский:** расходный; которым можно пожертвовать
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -able from *expend*

Able to be given up or used up, because it is not essential.

1. The workers felt *expendable*.
2. Gloves and masks are treated as *expendable* supplies.
3. In a budget cut, training is the first *expendable* item.

---

### expensive

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspɛnsɪv/ &middot; *ik-SPEN-siv*
**Русский:** дорогой, дорогостоящий
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more expensive*, *most expensive*
**Opposite:** *cheap*, *inexpensive*
**Contrast:** Things are *expensive*; people who spend a lot are *extravagant*. And a price is *high*, not *expensive*.

Costing a lot of money.

1. That's an *expensive* car for a first job.
2. Dinner downtown is *expensive*.
3. The repair turned out to be *more expensive* than the car was worth.

---

### experiential

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˌspɪriˈɛnʃəl/ &middot; *ik-speer-ee-EN-shuhl*
**Русский:** основанный на опыте, практический
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *experiential learning*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -al from *experience*

Based on doing something rather than on reading or hearing about it.

1. The program is built around *experiential* learning.
2. She studies the *experiential* side of memory.
3. The museum's new wing is fully *experiential*.

---

### experimental

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˌspɛrɪˈmɛntəl/ &middot; *ik-sper-uh-MEN-tuhl*
**Русский:** экспериментальный, опытный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more experimental*, *most experimental* (of art and music)
**Form:** -al from *experiment*

Experimental has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Still being tested, and not yet proven or approved.** (экспериментальный, опытный)

1. He enrolled in a trial of an *experimental* drug.
2. The treatment is still *experimental*.

**2. Using new and unusual methods, especially in art.** (экспериментальный, новаторский)

3. She writes *experimental* poetry.
4. Their second album is *more experimental* than the first.

---

### expert

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛkspɜːrt/ &middot; *EK-spurt*
**Русский:** опытный, квалифицированный; экспертный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *expert advice*
**Comparison:** more/most — *more expert*, *most expert*
**Takes:** *at* or *in* + noun or *-ing* (mainly with the noun: *an expert at fixing things*)
**Preposition patterns:** [expert at](../../06-prepositions/catalog/15-dependent-adjectives.md#expert-at)
**Contrast:** *An expert* is far more common as a noun. As an adjective it goes before the noun; say *she is an expert on birds*, not *she is expert*.

Having or showing great skill and knowledge.

1. We got *expert* advice from a tax attorney.
2. The piece was restored by *expert* hands.
3. He is the *most expert* witness they could find.

---

### expired

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspaɪərd/ &middot; *ik-SPY-urd*
**Русский:** просроченный, истёкший
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *expire*
**Contrast:** For food Americans say *expired* or *out of date*, never *overdue* — that is for library books and bills.

No longer valid, because the date on it has passed.

1. They wouldn't accept my *expired* driver's license.
2. Throw out the *expired* medication.
3. Your coupon is *expired*.

---

### explainable

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspleɪnəbəl/ &middot; *ik-SPLAY-nuh-buhl*
**Русский:** объяснимый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** more/most — *more explainable*, *most explainable*
**Takes:** *by* + noun
**Form:** *explain* + -able
**Opposite:** *inexplicable*

Able to be understood or accounted for.

1. Most of the difference is *explainable* by the weather.
2. The noise turned out to be easily *explainable*.
3. Researchers want *explainable* software, not a black box.

---

### explanatory

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsplænətɔːri/ &middot; *ik-SPLAN-uh-tor-ee*
**Русский:** пояснительный, объяснительный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *an explanatory note*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ory from *explain*
**Contrast:** Note the spelling: *explain* loses its *i* — *explanatory*, *explanation*.

Meant to make something clearer or easier to understand.

1. See the *explanatory* note at the bottom of the page.
2. The manual comes with *explanatory* diagrams.
3. A short *explanatory* video walks you through the form.

---

### explicit

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsplɪsɪt/ &middot; *ik-SPLIS-it*
**Русский:** явный, недвусмысленный; откровенный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more explicit*, *most explicit*
**Takes:** *about* + noun
**Opposite:** *implicit*

Explicit has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Said clearly and in detail, leaving no room for doubt.** (явный, недвусмысленный)

1. He gave *explicit* instructions not to be disturbed.
2. She was *explicit* about what she wanted.
3. Let me be *more explicit*: the answer is no.

**2. Showing sex or violence openly.** (откровенный)

4. The album carries an *explicit* lyrics warning.
5. Some scenes are sexually *explicit*.

---

### exploding

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsploʊdɪŋ/ &middot; *ik-SPLOH-ding*
**Русский:** взрывающийся; стремительно растущий
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *the exploding population*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *explode*

Exploding has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Blowing apart with great force.** (взрывающийся)

1. The movie ends with an *exploding* helicopter.
2. *Exploding* fireworks lit up the harbor.

**2. Growing very fast in number or size.** (стремительно растущий)

3. The city is struggling with an *exploding* population.
4. *Exploding* demand caught the factory off guard.

---

### exploitative

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsplɔɪtətɪv/ &middot; *ik-SPLOY-tuh-tiv*
**Русский:** эксплуататорский, хищнический
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exploitative*, *most exploitative*
**Form:** -ive from *exploit*

Treating people unfairly in order to profit from them.

1. The report describes *exploitative* labor practices.
2. Those interest rates are plainly *exploitative*.
3. The second contract was even *more exploitative* than the first.

---

### exploited

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsplɔɪtɪd/ &middot; *ik-SPLOY-tid*
**Русский:** эксплуатируемый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *exploit*

Used unfairly for someone else's profit.

1. *Exploited* farm workers often have no legal protection.
2. Interns felt *exploited* and quit.
3. She writes about *exploited* garment workers in Los Angeles.

---

### exploratory

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsplɔːrətɔːri/ &middot; *ik-SPLOR-uh-tor-ee*
**Русский:** исследовательский, пробный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *exploratory talks*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ory from *explore*

Done in order to find out more, before anything is decided.

1. The two sides held *exploratory* talks in Geneva.
2. The surgeon ordered *exploratory* surgery.
3. She formed an *exploratory* committee for a Senate run.

---

### explosive

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsploʊsɪv/ &middot; *ik-SPLOH-siv*
**Русский:** взрывчатый; взрывной, вспыльчивый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more explosive*, *most explosive*
**Form:** -ive from *explode*
**Contrast:** *Explosives* is also a plural noun — the material itself.

Explosive has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Able to blow up.** (взрывчатый)

1. Dogs are trained to sniff out *explosive* material.
2. The gas is highly *explosive*.

**2. Likely to cause sudden anger, argument or trouble.** (взрывоопасный, скандальный)

3. The testimony was *explosive*.
4. Race and money are the *most explosive* topics on the council.

**3. Sudden, fast and powerful.** (взрывной, стремительный)

5. The company saw *explosive* growth in its first year.
6. He's an *explosive* runner off the line.

---

### exponential

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛkspəˈnɛnʃəl/ &middot; *ek-spuh-NEN-shuhl*
**Русский:** экспоненциальный, стремительный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *exponential growth*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -al from *exponent*
**Contrast:** In math it is exact; in the newspaper it just means "very fast."

Growing faster and faster, by a larger amount each time.

1. The chart shows *exponential* growth in users.
2. Costs rose at an *exponential* rate.
3. There's been an *exponential* increase in applications.

---

### exposed

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspoʊzd/ &middot; *ik-SPOHZD*
**Русский:** открытый, незащищённый; обнажённый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exposed*, *most exposed*
**Takes:** *to* + noun
**Form:** -ed from *expose*

Exposed has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Not covered or protected from weather, danger or view.** (открытый, незащищённый)

1. The cabin sits on an *exposed* ridge.
2. Don't leave *exposed* wires where kids can reach them.
3. The north side of the house is *more exposed* to wind.

**2. Put in contact with something harmful.** (подвергшийся воздействию)

4. Workers were *exposed* to asbestos for years.
5. Anyone *exposed* to the virus should stay home.

---

### expository

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈspɑːzɪtɔːri/ &middot; *ik-SPOZ-i-tor-ee*
**Русский:** пояснительный, разъяснительный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** from *exposition*, related to *expose*

Explaining facts or ideas rather than telling a story or arguing.

1. The class teaches *expository* writing.
2. He cut the long *expository* passages from the script.
3. The first chapter is purely *expository*.

---

### express

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsprɛs/ &middot; *ik-SPRES*
**Русский:** срочный, экспресс-; ясно выраженный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *an express train*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *Express* is also a common verb (*express an opinion*) and a noun (*take the express*). The adjective only goes before a noun.

Express has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Fast, and stopping at few places or handled ahead of the rest.** (срочный, экспресс-)

1. Take the *express* train to Penn Station.
2. Ten items or fewer in the *express* lane.
3. Send it *express* mail if it has to be there Monday.

**2. Stated clearly and on purpose.** (ясно выраженный, прямой)

4. He came for the *express* purpose of apologizing.
5. You need her *express* permission in writing.

---

### expressionless

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsprɛʃənləs/ &middot; *ik-SPRESH-uhn-luhs*
**Русский:** бесстрастный, ничего не выражающий
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable — degree with *completely*: *completely expressionless*
**Form:** -less from *expression*
**Opposite:** *expressive*

Showing no feeling on the face or in the voice.

1. Her face stayed *expressionless* through the whole verdict.
2. He answered in a flat, *expressionless* voice.
3. The guard was completely *expressionless*.

---

### expressive

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsprɛsɪv/ &middot; *ik-SPRES-iv*
**Русский:** выразительный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more expressive*, *most expressive*
**Takes:** *of* + noun
**Form:** -ive from *express*
**Opposite:** *expressionless*

Showing thoughts and feelings clearly.

1. The baby has big, *expressive* eyes.
2. His playing is technically fine but not very *expressive*.
3. She is *more expressive* in her second language than in her first.

---

### exquisite

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈskwɪzɪt/ &middot; *ik-SKWIZ-it*
**Русский:** изысканный, утончённый
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exquisite*, *most exquisite*

Exquisite has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Extremely beautiful and delicately made.** (изысканный, утончённый)

1. She wore an *exquisite* silk dress.
2. The carving on the door is *exquisite*.
3. It was the *most exquisite* meal we had all year.

**2. Of a feeling, very strong and finely felt.** (острый, тонкий)

4. He has an *exquisite* sense of timing.
5. Waiting for the results was *exquisite* torture.

---

### extant

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛkstənt/ &middot; *EK-stuhnt*
**Русский:** сохранившийся, дошедший до нас
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Opposite:** *extinct*; *lost*
**Contrast:** Not *extinct*, its near-opposite: *extant* species are the ones still alive.

Still existing, especially after a long time.

1. Only three *extant* copies of the map are known.
2. The oldest *extant* building in town dates to 1789.
3. Few of his letters are still *extant*.

---

### extemporaneous

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˌstɛmpəˈreɪniəs/ &middot; *ik-stem-puh-RAY-nee-uhs*
**Русский:** импровизированный, без подготовки
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more extemporaneous*, *most extemporaneous*
**Opposite:** *prepared*
**Contrast:** Formal. Everyday English says *off the cuff*.

Spoken or done without preparation, made up as you go.

1. She gave an *extemporaneous* speech at the reception.
2. His remarks were entirely *extemporaneous*.
3. The debate tournament has an *extemporaneous* round.

---

### extendable

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstɛndəbəl/ &middot; *ik-STEN-duh-buhl*
**Русский:** раздвижной; продлеваемый
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -able from *extend*
**Contrast:** Also spelled *extendible*, but *extendable* is the usual American form.

Able to be made longer in size or in time.

1. The ladder is *extendable* to twenty feet.
2. The lease is *extendable* for another two years.
3. It comes with an *extendable* handle.

---

### extended

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstɛndɪd/ &middot; *ik-STEN-did*
**Русский:** продлённый, длительный; расширенный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *an extended stay*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *extend*
**Contrast:** *Extended family* means aunts, cousins and grandparents — not a big family.

Extended has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Longer than usual or than planned.** (продлённый, длительный)

1. He took an *extended* leave after the surgery.
2. The store has *extended* hours during the holidays.

**2. Including more than the usual core.** (расширенный)

3. Her *extended* family gathers every Thanksgiving.
4. The DVD has an *extended* version with twenty more minutes.

---

### extensive

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstɛnsɪv/ &middot; *ik-STEN-siv*
**Русский:** обширный, подробный; значительный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more extensive*, *most extensive*
**Form:** -ive from *extend*
**Contrast:** *Extensive* is about how much ground it covers; *intensive* is about how concentrated the effort is.

Covering a large area or a lot of material.

1. She has *extensive* experience in public health.
2. The library's collection is *extensive*.
3. The damage was *more extensive* than the first reports suggested.

---

### extenuating

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstɛnjueɪtɪŋ/ &middot; *ik-STEN-yoo-ay-ting*
**Русский:** смягчающий (вину, обстоятельства)
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun, almost always *circumstances*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *extenuate*
**Contrast:** It lives in one phrase: *extenuating circumstances*. You will rarely meet it anywhere else.

Making an offense seem less serious by explaining why it happened.

1. The judge found *extenuating* circumstances and reduced the sentence.
2. We can waive the fee if there are *extenuating* circumstances.
3. There were *extenuating* factors the report never mentioned.

---

### exterior

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstɪriər/ &middot; *ik-STEER-ee-ur*
**Русский:** внешний, наружный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *exterior walls*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Opposite:** *interior*
**Contrast:** *Exterior* is the outside surface of a thing (a house, a car); *external* is the wider word for anything outside (*external pressure*, *external hard drive*).

On or forming the outside of something, especially a building.

1. The *exterior* walls need paint.
2. Use *exterior* latex for the trim.
3. They shot the *exterior* scenes in Toronto.

---

### external

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstɜːrnəl/ &middot; *ik-STUR-nuhl*
**Русский:** внешний, наружный; сторонний
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Opposite:** *internal*

External has 2 distinct senses.

**1. On the outside, or coming from outside.** (внешний, наружный)

1. The label says "for *external* use only."
2. I back everything up to an *external* drive.

**2. Belonging to something outside the group or organization.** (сторонний, внешний)

3. The board hired an *external* auditor.
4. The pressure was *external*, not from anyone on the team.

---

### extinct

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstɪŋkt/ &middot; *ik-STINGKT*
**Русский:** вымерший, исчезнувший; потухший
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable — a species either is or isn't
**Opposite:** *extant*
**Contrast:** *Extinct* (died out) and *extinguished* (put out, of a fire) come from the same root but are not interchangeable.

Extinct has 2 distinct senses.

**1. No longer existing, of a species or a way of life.** (вымерший, исчезнувший)

1. The passenger pigeon has been *extinct* since 1914.
2. Several frog species have gone *extinct* in the last decade.
3. Pay phones are all but *extinct*.

**2. Of a volcano, no longer active and not expected to erupt again.** (потухший)

4. The island sits on an *extinct* volcano.
5. Geologists say the crater is *extinct*, not dormant.

---

### extortionate

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstɔːrʃənət/ &middot; *ik-STOR-shuh-nuht*
**Русский:** грабительский, непомерный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more extortionate*, *most extortionate*
**Form:** -ate from *extortion*

Of a price or rate, so high that it feels like robbery.

1. The airport charges *extortionate* parking rates.
2. Payday lenders charge *extortionate* interest.
3. Their fee is *more extortionate* than anything I've seen.

---

### extra

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛkstrə/ &middot; *EK-struh*
**Русский:** дополнительный, лишний, запасной
**Type:** quantity
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *Extra* is also an adverb (*extra large*, *extra carefully*) and a noun (*an extra* in a movie). Note *an extra chair*, never *a extra chair*.

More than the usual or expected number or amount.

1. Bring an *extra* pair of socks.
2. Guacamole is *extra*.
3. She worked *extra* hours all through December.

---

### extra-large

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛkstrəˈlɑːrdʒ/ &middot; *ek-struh-LARJ*
**Русский:** очень большой, размера XL
**Type:** size
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — *extra* + *large*, abbreviated XL

Bigger than the large size.

1. Do you have this shirt in *extra-large*?
2. He ordered an *extra-large* pizza for four people.
3. The recipe calls for two *extra-large* eggs.

---

### extracurricular

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛkstrəkəˈrɪkjələr/ &middot; *ek-struh-kuh-RIK-yuh-lur*
**Русский:** внеклассный, внеучебный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *extracurricular activities*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** extra- (outside) + *curricular*
**Contrast:** Jokingly used for an affair: *his extracurricular activities*.

Done at school but outside regular classes.

1. Colleges look at *extracurricular* activities too.
2. Band and debate are her main *extracurricular* interests.
3. Budget cuts hit the *extracurricular* programs first.

---

### extrajudicial

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛkstrədʒuːˈdɪʃəl/ &middot; *eks-truh-joo-DISH-uhl*
**Русский:** внесудебный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** *extra-* "outside" + *judicial*

Done outside the courts and without their authority.

1. The report documents *extrajudicial* killings.
2. The families reached an *extrajudicial* settlement.
3. *Extrajudicial* detention is banned under the treaty.

---

### extramarital

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛkstrəˈmɛrɪtəl/ &middot; *ek-struh-MER-i-tuhl*
**Русский:** внебрачный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *an extramarital affair*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** extra- (outside) + *marital*

Happening outside a marriage, with someone who is not your husband or wife.

1. He admitted to an *extramarital* affair.
2. The book studies *extramarital* relationships across cultures.
3. Rumors of an *extramarital* romance ended his campaign.

---

### extraneous

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstreɪniəs/ &middot; *ik-STRAY-nee-uhs*
**Русский:** посторонний, лишний, не относящийся к делу
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Takes:** *to* + noun

Not connected with what is being dealt with, and therefore unnecessary.

1. Cut the *extraneous* detail from the introduction.
2. That argument is *extraneous* to the case.
3. *Extraneous* noise ruined the recording.

---

### extraordinary

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstrɔːrdənɛri/ &middot; *ik-STROR-duh-ner-ee*
**Русский:** необыкновенный, выдающийся; чрезвычайный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more extraordinary*, *most extraordinary*
**Opposite:** *ordinary*
**Contrast:** Stress falls on the second syllable, and the *a* of *extra* almost disappears: ik-STROR-duh-ner-ee, not "extra-ordinary."

Extraordinary has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Far beyond what is usual, in a way that surprises or impresses.** (необыкновенный, выдающийся)

1. She has an *extraordinary* memory for names.
2. What he did that night was *extraordinary*.
3. It was the *most extraordinary* thing I've ever seen.

**2. Arranged for a special purpose, outside the normal schedule.** (чрезвычайный, внеочередной)

4. The board called an *extraordinary* meeting.
5. The envoy is an ambassador *extraordinary*.

---

### extrasensory

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛkstrəˈsɛnsəri/ &middot; *ek-struh-SEN-suh-ree*
**Русский:** экстрасенсорный, сверхчувственный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun, nearly always *perception*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** extra- (beyond) + *sensory*

Coming from outside the five normal senses.

1. The lab tested claims of *extrasensory* perception.
2. She says she has some kind of *extrasensory* ability.
3. There is no solid evidence for *extrasensory* powers.

---

### extraterrestrial

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɛkstrətəˈrɛstriəl/ &middot; *ek-struh-tuh-RES-tree-uhl*
**Русский:** внеземной, инопланетный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** extra- (beyond) + *terrestrial* (of the earth)
**Contrast:** Also a noun, shortened to *ET*: *the search for extraterrestrials*.

Coming from, or existing, outside the earth.

1. They scan the sky for *extraterrestrial* signals.
2. No *extraterrestrial* life has ever been found.
3. The rock turned out to be *extraterrestrial* in origin.

---

### extreme

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstriːm/ &middot; *ik-STREEM*
**Русский:** крайний, чрезвычайный; экстремальный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more extreme*, *most extreme*
**Opposite:** *moderate*
**Contrast:** *Extremes* is also a noun: *go to extremes*, *the extremes of the desert*.

Extreme has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Very great in degree, far beyond the normal.** (крайний, чрезвычайный)

1. He was in *extreme* pain.
2. Use it only in an *extreme* emergency.
3. This winter was *more extreme* than any in living memory.

**2. Of opinions, far from what most people think, and often dangerous.** (крайний, радикальный)

4. He holds *extreme* political views.
5. That's an *extreme* position, even for him.

**3. Of sports, dangerous and exciting.** (экстремальный)

6. She's into *extreme* sports — base jumping, mostly.
7. The channel shows *extreme* skiing all winter.

---

### extremist

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈstriːmɪst/ &middot; *ik-STREE-mist*
**Русский:** экстремистский
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only — before a noun: *extremist groups*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ist from *extreme*
**Contrast:** *An extremist* is also a noun, and the commoner use: *the group was founded by extremists*.

Holding political or religious views far outside the mainstream, often with violence.

1. The site was taken down for hosting *extremist* content.
2. He was drawn into an *extremist* group online.
3. *Extremist* violence rose sharply that year.

---

### extrinsic

**Pronunciation:** /ɛkˈstrɪnsɪk/ &middot; *ek-STRIN-sik*
**Русский:** внешний; не присущий по природе
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** the opposite of *intrinsic*
**Opposite:** *intrinsic*

Coming from outside a person or thing, not part of its basic nature.

1. Grades and money are *extrinsic* rewards; curiosity is an intrinsic one.
2. The value of a dollar bill is *extrinsic* — the paper itself is worth almost nothing.
3. Researchers looked at both genetic and *extrinsic* factors.

---

### extroverted

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛkstrəˌvɜːrtɪd/ &middot; *EK-struh-vur-tid*
**Русский:** общительный, экстравертный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more extroverted*, *most extroverted*
**Form:** -ed from *extrovert*; psychologists also spell it *extraverted*
**Opposite:** *introverted*

Outgoing and full of energy around other people; enjoying company.

1. She's very *extroverted* and loves meeting new people.
2. My *extroverted* brother talks to everyone on the bus.
3. I'm *more extroverted* at work than I am at home.

---

### exuberant

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzuːbərənt/ &middot; *ig-ZOO-buh-ruhnt*
**Русский:** жизнерадостный, полный энергии; буйный, пышный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more exuberant*, *most exuberant*
